"Morning," Rowe said, and something in his voice on that one word sat differently than it had on the other two, though Orim could not yet have said in what particular way - only that he noted the difference, in the place he kept things he intended to return to, and did not return to it yet.

The fourth word came immediately after, flat, in the tone of a thing held in the mouth too long and needing, finally, to leave it.

"Gone."

Rowe sat with his hands on his knees, looking at the four words on the page, and Orim watched him decide something - a man's hands generally confess before his face does, in Orim's long experience of both, faces having had more practice at composure.

*Factory. Smoke. Morning. Gone.*

"That's the whole of it," Rowe said. "Tonight."

There was more sentence than that, and all three of them in the hold knew it. Four words handed over the way a man hands over a single photograph from a stack he has no intention of showing the rest of - enough to prove the stack exists, no more.

"A factory," Orim said, at last, looking not at Rowe but at the four words on the page, the way a man looks at a lock rather than at the person he's asking to open it. "Yours."

Not a question. He left it room to be answered like one anyway, the way he left most of his statements room, because a door forced open teaches a man less than a door he believes he opened himself.

Rowe nodded once. Not resistance. The specific tension of a man confirming something he had been trying, in his own economy, to say for longer than this conversation had existed.

"You came from there," Orim said, very slowly, and did not point at anything, because he did not need to.

"Yes."

Orim let that sit in the hold a long moment, unremarked, the way a man lets a fact settle before deciding what shelf it belongs on. He had what he had come for. Whether Rowe understood that this had been, from the first cask question three days back, precisely the shape of thing Orim had been fishing toward - that was a separate matter, and one Orim did not resolve for him, that night or any other.

[Beat 113]
He didn't sleep again that night, and did not trouble himself over the lack of it; sleep had stopped being owed to him at an age well behind him now, and he had made his peace with the arrears.

Ardea came below at first light to count the provisions, looked at Lyra, then at Rowe - idled somewhere around the fifth hour, hands finally open - then at Orim, with the expression of a woman who had several questions and had correctly decided none of them were hers to ask.

"We'll see Atrament inside of four hours," Ardea said, to no one in particular.

"I know," Orim said, before Lyra could.

Ardea accepted this with the professional grace of a woman who recognized when the answer had already been decided by someone senior to her watch rotation, and went to count provisions.

Orim made the cup of something he made when he judged a person needed warming and did not want to make a performance of caring about it - not quite tea, not quite anything else - and handed it down to Lyra without comment. She drank it. He watched her decide, from the taste, that it was bad, the way everyone who had ever drunk it from him eventually decided, and said nothing to spare either of them the argument.

"Four words," she said.

"I heard." He had done rather more than hear them, though he did not correct the verb.

"You were supposed to be sleeping."

"I was listening. It's different." He settled his back against the hull. His joints cracked when he moved; he had stopped noticing the sound himself years before this crossing, though he understood it still unsettled people new to him. "Four words that go together like a sentence."

"The shape of a sentence. I don't have the rest of it yet."

"Factory," he said. "Smoke. Morning. Gone."

"Yes."

He was quiet a moment, weighing how much of the next thing was worth handing over, in exactly the manner he had spent three days teaching Rowe to weigh the same question, without ever once naming the resemblance aloud. "In my first contracted Scry session, the membrane I was put in contact with was the size of my palm. I was told it was a developed specimen. An old one. They didn't tell me what that meant until later." He looked out at the river. "I was given nineteen questions to hold in mind during contact. I managed twelve before the session was called. The guild recorder filed the results immediately; I was not permitted to review them." He paused, the pause doing most of the work the sentence around it declined to do. "Standard practice. I never found it strange until recently."

Lyra said nothing.

"The notation on the manifest," he said. "The same as his rifle."

"Yes."

"Someone documented that sphere very thoroughly." He did not say *before.* He did not say *before it burned.* He looked at the porthole, then at Rowe's idled shape, then at the manifest in her lap. "Thorough documentation. Filed in the commercial freight system. Sitting between grain and wine in every barge manifest going north for-" He stopped himself, the way a man stops on a step he has just noticed is rotted. "How long does a guild archive hold its records?"

"Sixty years," she said. "Older than that, they're at the central repository in Verlaine."

"Sixty years," he said. "At minimum." He turned back to the glass. "Good documentation," he said, in the tone he used for things that were not good at all - a tone he had been using, on and off, his entire career, for the same handful of things, and had never once, to date, used correctly in front of anyone who could have done something about it.

[Beat 114]
The sky changed before the smell did.

Orim noticed it first, the way he noticed most changes in this stretch of sky, having crossed it enough times that changes announced themselves to him the way a familiar face announces a new scar - the gray shifted in tone rather than temperature, a faint yellowish cast, as though the air between the barge and the horizon had been filtered through something warmer and sustained and industrial before it reached the glass. He said nothing about it. There was nothing useful to say about a thing everyone aboard was about to smell for themselves inside the half hour.

The smell arrived twenty minutes later. Coal first - not coal in storage, cold and damp, but coal that had burned long enough to pass through all its stages and settle into the char-and-mineral note of a furnace in continuous operation. Behind it, rendered fat, tallow reduced to its parts for candle and lubricant. Behind that, something sharper: hot metal, the mineral edge Orim had catalogued the first time he'd made this passage, decades back now, and filed as *Atrament entry signature, industrial, note for future reference.* He noted it again. It sat in the same category it had always sat in. It did not sit with the same weight.

Rowe came up from his corner when the smell changed, at the porthole before anyone turned to look for him - not crowding, simply present, the way he was present whenever something demanded it of him. The ticking under his plating had slowed. His face was doing the thing it did over the manifest, the door blown briefly open, closed again before an inventory could be taken.

The sound arrived before the stacks did. Orim felt it in his sternum first, the way he'd felt it every crossing for longer than he cared to count precisely - a low continuous vibration, not rhythmic like the rail yards but constant, the acoustic signature of processes that do not pause for dark or morning or season. It separated, as the barge drew closer, into its parts: the percussion of the forges, regular as a pulse; a higher mechanical whine that had run long enough to find its frequency and settle there; and beneath both, a ground-note that was less a sound than a pressure against the ribs, the kind of thing a body registers before the ears do.

Then the stacks appeared at the river's bend - dark verticals against the yellowed sky, eight of them, then nine, then more as the bend opened onto the full waterfront. Taller than Orim remembered, or the same height and he was only reading them differently now, carrying different things than he had the last time. The smoke coming off them was the color of the sky itself, already absorbed into it, indistinguishable at any distance he could measure, moving south the same direction the barge was moving, so that no one aboard could have said with any confidence where the stacks ended and the sky began.

Rowe stood very still at the glass.

Orim did not need to see the man's face to know its shape. He had the words for the sentence already; he had built three days toward exactly this arrival, one cask and one small patient question at a time, and whether that building had been done in Rowe's interest, or in some other interest entirely - the Vigil's, the Entos's, his own, some ledger only Orim kept and had kept for forty years without once opening it for outside inspection - was not a question Orim answered for himself either, standing at that porthole, watching the fifth word arrive before anyone had asked for it.

Lyra held the notebook up to Rowe, open to the four words, the way a person holds up a door they are not asking someone to walk through, only to notice.

He looked at the four words. He looked at the stacks. Something in him that had been carefully, professionally held for a long time went briefly uncontrolled - Orim watched it happen with the same attention he gave everything, and felt, for exactly as long as it lasted, considerably older than his years rather than merely as old as them - and then controlled itself again. He took the pen and, without being asked, wrote a fifth word beneath the others, small and precise and entirely legible.

*Home.*

Nobody asked him to explain it. Orim filed it in the place he kept the things he had no intention of explaining either, and found, not for the first time this crossing, that the place had gotten considerably more crowded than he generally allowed it to get.

The stacks kept coming. The smell thickened. Orim stood at the porthole of a barge called the *Houndstooth*, watching a city he had crossed to more times than he'd troubled to count arrive out of a yellow sky the way it always arrived, and did not tell anyone in the hold - not Lyra, not Rowe, not, this particular morning, himself - which part of the last three days he had spent drawing the man out for his own good, and which part he had spent, the way he had spent most of the last forty years, simply watching to see what the water gave up on its own.

[Beat 115]
The *Houndstooth* put them off at the Atrament wharf an hour past dawn. Lyra logged the hour the way she logged everything: against the light, not the bell, because bells in a foreign port could not be trusted and the sun could. Ardea stayed at the customs shed to fight the barge master's manifest down to a figure the dockmaster would stamp without opening the main hold. Lyra had watched her do this in six ports now. It was a discipline like any other — patience, a documented willingness to yield at the exact moment yielding cost least, and the specific skill of looking beaten while winning. Ardea was good at it. She would need an hour. Possibly more.

Lyra left Rowe at the edge of the customs square with an order in the Entos he was learning well enough now to take orders in — *watch, do not be seen to watch* — and walked the harbor front alone, the way she walked any perimeter before she trusted it.

This was not the Atrament she had heard men describe in taverns further south, the Forge Kingdom of ordered stone and gearwork precision. This was its back. The side that faced the river and dealt with the outside world, and so wore none of the interior's polish. Stevedores. Customs men. A fish seller working a flat boat off the pilings. The iron-filing smell she had come to associate with Atrament foundries, carried on air too cold for the season by a week she'd have put money on. Everything here was built for work and judged by nothing else. She approved of it, in the detached way she approved of a wall that did its job and asked no praise for doing it.

[Beat 116]
Orim was at the fish stall. He had not been aboard the *Houndstooth*. He had not, to her knowledge, been in Bressant. He was eating smoked river fish and watching the harbor with the loose, unhurried attention of a man with nowhere to be — a posture she had learned, over the length of this campaign, to distrust on sight.

"You should have been three days behind me," she said.

"I was," he said. "Then I wasn't."

He'd taken the western pass instead of the river — faster in the thaw, he said, and he'd spent a summer in these uplands sixty years past for reasons he did not presently care to itemize, and had kept the shortcut filed since. He said this the way a man reports the price of sardines. She logged it in the ledger she kept for all of Orim's accountings: *true. incomplete. sufficient for present purposes.* It was not trust. It was the closest thing to trust that worked.

He had also wanted a day in Atrament ahead of her arrival.

"For what?" she asks.

"To ask questions," he said. "People answer honestly in the day before a party arrives. Once you're standing in front of them, they remember you'll hear it, and revise."

She marked that too. Extremely useful. Possibly extremely unsettling. The file did not require her to choose.

[Beat 117]
Back at the square, Rowe had not shifted from where she'd set him — which she noted the way she noted a picket holding position without being told twice. Three men had taken up the far end in the specific arrangement of watchers who have decided to watch something without yet deciding what they mean to do about it. Two wore the Atrament forge-mark on the left pauldron, army issue, stamping worn but present. The third had cut his away — the thing a man did in the Forge Kingdom when the army and he parted on terms neither side cared to put in writing.

She had seen the type in a dozen garrison towns. Men who'd walked out of one war and now stood at the seam of another, waiting to learn if it paid better than the last one had. She did not look at them straight on. She looked at Rowe instead, at the blank plane of his face, and found he wasn't looking at them either. He was watching a flat-bottomed river runner at the far end of the wharf, bow low, a crew moving cargo up from the hold in the patient, over-long rhythm of men working past the end of a shift that should have ended.

"What do you see?" she asks, arriving at his shoulder.

"Eleven," he said. A pause. "Not crew."

She counted for herself. Cargo hands, the manifest team at the gangway, the clerk with his board, and two men holding the forward dock lines who were dressed for nothing that required dock lines. Eleven, if you counted right. It came out right.

She looked back at the three men on the square. They were watching the same ship.

"Them?" she asks.

"Counting," Rowe said.

She filed the square, the ship, and the three watchers under a single heading — *not our engagement, logged, do not close with* — and turned to find Orim already at her left shoulder. She had not heard him arrive. She noted that too, and moved on, because a woman who stopped to be unsettled by every unsettling thing on this road would never get anywhere.

[Beat 118]
"The fish stall was not purely for the fish," he said, in the tone he used for things he expected her to have already worked out herself.

"It faces the dock."

"A man who sells fish at dawn has seen everything that moved in the night. Oldest information exchange in any port that has one." He folded his hands into his coat. "Four ships ran the northern route this winter. One went missing in the passes. Verlaine's garrison logged it *administrative loss* and declined to follow up."

She knew the phrase. She'd read it once in a procurement review and hadn't followed it up either. She filed that admission separately, where she kept the things about herself she did not care to examine twice.

"That ship."

"Perhaps. The fish seller couldn't read the hull marks. He noted the paint, though. Fresh. Low, below the waterline. Put on in the dark."

Ardea reached them from the customs shed, stamped manifests in hand, wearing the look of a woman who'd paid four times a fair price and meant to file it as a cost of doing business. She looked at Lyra, then the ship, then Rowe.

"What did I miss?" she asks.

"The fish stall," Lyra said. "And eleven men who aren't crew."

Ardea looked. "I count twelve."

Four of them stood at the edge of a Forge Kingdom wharf that smelled of iron and river water, each arriving at a different number, none of the numbers agreeing. Lyra did not find this reassuring. A working count should hold across four separate pairs of eyes. When it didn't, that was itself the finding.

"We're not staying," she said.

"No," Orim agreed.

They left by the southern gate — not because it was safer, precisely, but because the twelve men weren't there, and because whatever the ship was or wasn't did not require an opinion from any of them. The highland road lay three hours north. It had been well-marked for three hundred years and ran exactly where they needed to go, which in Lyra's experience was the most a road ever owed you.

Rowe walked it without looking back. She had noticed that about him before. Men who look back are still deciding whether to be here. Rowe had decided some while ago, and simply hadn't mentioned when.

[Beat 119]
The transit stamp Verlaine's border post had pressed into her papers three days south was, Orim informed her somewhere in the second hour, worth exactly nothing past the next valley. She had suspected as much and asked anyway, because a fact you've had confirmed twice is a fact you can act on without the half-second of doubt that gets people killed at checkpoints.

"The line moved," Orim said. "Then it moved back. Then it moved again, further than the first time. This valley's changed hands four times in thirty years, by the marker stones. The village at the crossing has been *liberated*" — he gave the word no particular weight, which was its own kind of weight — "by both Houses, more than once each. They believe neither anymore. Why would they? A garrison that frees you and a garrison that frees you back are, from where the village stands, the same event happening twice."

She understood this the way she understood any front: not as a line on a map but as a fact that expired. A stamp was proof of passage on the day it was pressed, in the place it was pressed, and nowhere else. Beyond that it was paper. She had crossed enough contested ground to know that the only durable fact on a shifting front was the shifting itself.

The road bore that out within the hour. Refugees moving both directions past each other without acknowledgment, which told her more than either group's papers would have. A cart with Verlaine markings painted over in a color that wasn't quite managing to be Atrament's. A boy of perhaps ten, watching them pass with the flat, cataloguing look of a child who has learned to note which uniform is on which men before he decides whether to run.

[Beat 120]
And the deserters. More of them the further they went, drifting the verges in twos and threes, the way water finds low ground. She'd started sorting them without meaning to, the way she sorted anything moving in numbers on a road she meant to keep using.

Some carried the stillness she recognized from Rowe on his worse days — men whose eyes tracked correctly and whose hands did not, or the reverse, a wrongness in the wiring that no drill she knew had a name for. Verlaine's product. Minds taken up whole into their war-constructs and returned, when they were returned at all, not quite refitted to the skull that had grown around them. Myrmidon strays. The word had come to her from Orim without her asking where he'd learned it, which she had also filed and not asked about.

Others bore a different mark: a stiffness at the base of the skull, a raised ridge under the collar where something had been socketed and then, with more violence than care, unsocketed. Rig-pilots who had cut their own couplings and walked. Atrament's word for these, when the road bothered to have a word at all, was *war-dogs* — not affectionately.

She watched Rowe watch them. He did not slow. He did not speak. But something in the set of his shoulders eased by a measurable degree each time they passed a stray moving the other way, and tightened again once the distance closed back past recognition range. She understood this without needing it explained to her. A soldier reads rank on a stranger's collar before he reads the face under it. Rowe was reading something else on these men — not rank. Kind. He was among his own, on a road that had no country for either of them, and he knew it before she did.

She did not comment on it. Some things a commanding officer notes and does not require a report on. She filed it under *understood, no action required*, and kept walking.

[Beat 121]
The waystation came up out of the dusk the way waystations on contested roads always did — squat, stone, built by whichever House had held the ground last with an eye to defense first and comfort as an afterthought that had mostly not survived the drafting stage. A signboard hung crooked on one chain, the paint long since weathered past reading either House's colors into it. That, Lyra thought, was probably deliberate, and probably the smartest decision anyone on this road had made all week.

Inside it was packed to the doorframe, and packed with exactly the traffic the road had been showing her all day, concentrated now into one low room with one fire and one bar and nowhere for any of it to disperse. Strays at one end, their stillness a held note under the general noise. War-dogs at the other, drinking the way men drink who've spent a great deal of effort making sure a coupling can never again be forced back into the base of their skull. Between them, everyone else — teamsters, a pair of Bressant traders, a woman who from her boots and posture had walked out of some third war entirely and wanted no part of these two.

Two Houses' worth of deserters, still technically at war with each other, drinking under the same roof because the alternative was the cold. Lyra logged the room the way she logged any ground before she committed troops to holding it: exits — one front, one through the kitchen she could smell but not see; ceiling height — low, which favored close work and disfavored anyone who liked room to swing; density — bad. Very bad. If this went wrong it would go wrong at arm's length, on all sides at once.

Ardea got them a table near the kitchen door, which Lyra noted and approved of without saying so. Orim was gone within a minute of sitting, which she also noted and did not chase. He would surface with something. He always did.

Rowe sat with his back to the wall, which she had not told him to do. He had started doing it three weeks ago without her instruction, and she had stopped being surprised by it two weeks ago. She watched him watch the room the way the strays at the far end were watching it — not fear, nothing so simple as fear. Assessment. The habit of a man for whom every room had once, not so long ago, been a room he might be ordered to die in.

[Beat 122]
The spark, when it came, was exactly as small as sparks on grounds like this always were.

A war-dog three tables over — young, the coupling-ridge under his collar still raised and pink, cut recently enough that he was still finding out what his own neck did without the socket in it — stood too fast reaching for a second mug and put his elbow through the arm of a stray behind him, who did not flinch, because the stray's arm had not, for reasons Lyra suspected went back to whatever Verlaine had done to the wiring behind it, fully belonged to him in the way an arm should. The mug went over. The drink went down the front of the war-dog's coat, over the Atrament forge-mark he had, unlike his two friends outside the wharf that morning, not cut away — a sentimental holdover, she'd have guessed, from a service he hated and hadn't quite finished hating.

"Watch your—" the war-dog started, and turned, and saw what he'd hit the arm of, and stopped mid-word, because now he could see the stray's face and the stray's face had the wrongness on it that these roads had taught every soldier, deserter or otherwise, to read at a glance.

"Verlaine trash," he said. Not loud. Loud enough.

The stray did not answer with words. Something in his jaw set, the mechanical evenness of a reflex nobody had quite trained out of him, and his chair went back.

Lyra was on her feet before the chair finished moving. Not from alarm. From arithmetic. A room this dense, with this traffic, at this density, did not survive one man standing with intent. It survived, if it survived at all, on someone closing the gap before the second man stood.

She did not reach the gap in time. That was the honest accounting of it, and she filed it as such even mid-motion: too far, too dense, the tables working against her the way tables in a packed room always did. The war-dog's friend — older, steadier, the kind of steady that came from having done this exact thing in an actual engagement — was up and swinging a stool at the stray's head before Lyra had covered half the distance.

After that it was not a fight so much as a room deciding, all at once and without consultation, that it had been waiting all evening for permission.

[Beat 123]
She took the nearest hand that reached for her — a teamster, she registered, not even a party to the original grievance, just a man whose instinct in a collapsing room was to grab whatever moved closest — and did to it what regulation called a standing wrist lock and what her body did without her needing to name it first: rotated the wrist past the joint's honest range, applied weight through the elbow, and put him face-first onto the table he'd been sitting at a moment before. It cost him the wrist for a fortnight, not the arm, which she counted a mercy given how little time she'd had to be merciful in.

A second man came at her from the flank — a stray, moving with that same overcorrected evenness, throwing a punch that had all the force of a soldier's fist and none of a soldier's aim, because whatever had been done to him had not left him the timing to go with the strength. She stepped inside it rather than back from it — the correct answer to a slow, strong strike was always to close the distance, never to retreat from it — and drove her forearm up under his, using his own committed weight to fold him over her shoulder and down. His skull found the table edge on the way. She heard the sound it made and did not stop to assess the damage, because assessing damage mid-engagement was how you acquired more of your own.

Somewhere to her left the coupling-ridge war-dog and the original stray were on the floor, grappling in the specific graceless way of men who have both, at some point, been trained to kill efficiently and have both since lost whatever discipline made that training worth anything. She left them to each other for the four seconds it took to deal with the third man closing on Ardea's flank — a Bressant trader, drunk enough to have decided this was somehow his fight — a single strike to the side of the knee, not the killing angle, just the one that put a man on the ground wondering what had happened to his leg. He went down loud and stayed down, which was the desired outcome, whatever the noise cost her.

Rowe had not moved from the wall. She registered this in the same half-second sweep that took in everything else, and understood it for what it was: he was watching the strays, not the war-dogs, and he was not moving because whatever calculation he was running about his own kind was one she did not have time to interrupt.

[Beat 124]
The two men on the floor — war-dog and stray, forge-mark and wrongness — had stopped grappling and started actually fighting, which was worse, and it was this pair Lyra closed on last and dealt with first in terms of consequence. She took the war-dog's raised arm as it came up for a strike that would have opened the stray's face on the table leg, and did the thing regulation called a standing armbar and her instructors had called, with the particular grim humor of people who taught this for a living, *the polite way to end an argument*. Elbow past its honest range, weight through the shoulder, and the joint went with a sound like a green branch breaking underfoot — not loud, wetter than loud, the specific sound a body makes when it fails at a task it was never built to refuse.

He screamed. That was the honest cost of it, and she did not pretend otherwise to herself. A broken arm on a man who would need that arm to work, to fight, to hold a mug in a country that hadn't stopped being at war just because he'd walked out of it. She had traded that cost against the alternative — an opened face, possibly a dead man, definitely a longer fight — and she stood by the trade the way she stood by every trade made under those terms: not gladly. Correctly.

The room did not resolve into silence so much as into exhaustion. Fights of this kind did not end with one side victorious. They ended when there was no longer enough will left standing to continue them. Within another ten seconds it had guttered out on its own — men disengaging not because anyone had won anything but because the will to keep swinging had simply run out, the way water runs out of a cracked jug, no single dramatic failure, just an emptying.

She counted the cost the way she counted everything: two men down and likely to stay down past tonight — the war-dog with the arm, and the trader with the knee, who would walk, but not soon and not without complaint. A stray with a split scalp bleeding freely onto a floor already sticky with spilled drink, who accepted a rag from the barkeep without thanks or apparent awareness that thanks were customary. Broken crockery. One table with a leg gone entirely, canted against the wall like a man favoring an injury. No one had died. That was the whole of the victory available in a room like this, and she took it, because it was the only version on offer.

[Beat 125]
Ardea was already at the bar working the barkeep down to a figure for the damages, which struck Lyra, even mid-recovery, as the single most reassuring sight in the room — Ardea doing the exact patient, willing-to-yield-at-the-right-moment work she'd been doing at the customs shed that morning, as though a wharf negotiation and a tavern brawl's aftermath were the same discipline wearing two coats. In her accounting they probably were.

Orim reappeared from wherever he had gone — the kitchen, she guessed, from the smell of woodsmoke freshly on him — and surveyed the wreckage with the same unhurried attention he'd given the harbor that morning.

"You'll want to know," he said, "that the boy with the broken arm was Verlaine-marked under the collar three months ago, before he cut and joined the Atrament line as a rig-pilot instead. Neither House would claim him now. He told me this himself, between the second scream and the third." He said it without particular sympathy, which was, she had come to understand, simply how Orim reported facts that did not admit of comfortable framing.

"And the stray."

"Doesn't know his own name. Hasn't for eleven months. The others feed him and tell him where to walk. It is, I am given to understand, a common enough courtesy on this road that it has its own word among them." He did not supply the word. She did not ask for it.

[Beat 126]
She looked once more across the room — the strays reassembling at their end with the same unhurried stillness as before, as though the last two minutes had happened to some other room entirely; the war-dogs doing the same at theirs, minus one now on the floor with his arm bound crudely by a man who clearly had field experience setting them. Rowe still hadn't moved from the wall. His eyes, though, had found the reassembled strays and stayed there a moment longer than assessment required.

She did not ask him what he saw in them. She thought she already knew, and a commanding officer did not make a man report aloud what she could read plainly enough on his own face: that he was watching a room full of what he might have become, had the Verlaine program worked on him the way it was meant to instead of the way it had. That the men at that end of the room were not strangers to him in any sense that mattered. That there was, on a road with no country for either side of it, exactly one kind of company he recognized as his own, and it was this — silent, broken, minding each other's names for men who'd lost them.

She had, she noted, stopped saying the crossing-prayer somewhere around the second contested border. She could not now recall exactly which one. She filed the absence the same way she filed everything else that no longer held — not as loss, precisely. As an entry that had simply gone quiet, the way a garrison's logbook goes quiet on a front that has stopped being anyone's to hold.

They paid Ardea's negotiated figure, which was, predictably, four times fair and treated by all present as the cost of doing business. They left before full dark, because a room that had already proven it could go over once could go over twice, and Lyra had no interest in confirming the theory.

The highland road took them on into the dark toward the Atrament interior, three hundred years well-marked and running, as ever, exactly where they needed to go. Rowe walked it the way he'd walked the wharf road that morning — without looking back — and this time she understood the habit for what it plainly was, and did not need Orim to explain it to her first.

[Beat 127]
The sound was wrong before anything else was, and Lyra had it in her boots three hours up the highland road before her ears agreed to confirm what her feet already knew.

The rail line ran below them in the valley, curving south around the granite bluff before swinging north again toward the Schmalburg basin. The road they walked ran above it, and older — older than the rail, older than the Compact itself by the evidence of its own construction: dressed stone in courses, the mortar long gone gray and crumbling at the joints, the stone itself still seated true. This was the quality of the old work on the major approaches, built by people who had meant to move armies rather than goods, who had not planned for the day the armies would be replaced by freight schedules. The road held well enough under the weight of four people who were not a siege.

Not weather. The sky over the northern highlands sat flat white with high autumn overcast, the kind that holds no storm and only promises the cold will get worse before it improves. What she felt was a deep, continuous vibration coming from somewhere ahead and below, a sustained bass note the body registered before the ears would confirm it. She'd had it moving up through the stone under her boots for the better part of a quarter hour before she understood it wasn't her own fatigue.

"We're getting close," Orim said.

He was ahead of her on the road, moving in the steady, unhurried way of a man who had covered a great deal of ground over fifty years and made his peace with the pace it cost. He had not looked back. He had not slowed.

"You've been here before."

"I have crossed the Atrament border in eight directions over forty years," he said. "The sound is the same in all of them. You stop hearing it after the second day. Then you leave, and you stop not-hearing it, and the quiet sounds wrong for a week." He stepped around a gap in the stone facing where a spring had colonized the joint. "Or so I'm told. I've never stayed long enough for the second part."

Lyra had her own crossing marked on paper three days behind them — the transit stamp from the Ocipheus liaison post, dated, countersigned, current as of the hour it was cut. She had assumed it would hold for the length of the valley. It would not. The border on the Compact's own maps sat a fixed line; the border on the ground sat wherever the last exchange of ground had left it, and that, by the wear on the verge markers, had been some weeks past. It would sit somewhere else again before the ink on her papers had finished drying in this cold. A transit stamp was a fact about a morning. It was not a fact about a war, and this was a war, whatever the Compact's filings called it on the days it didn't want to.

The smell came next. Not the smell of any one thing yet — the category of it. Metal worked past the temperature it wanted to be worked at. Stone asked to survive conditions stone was never built for. Something chemical underneath both that coated the back of the throat without being quite unpleasant, the smell of a place that had been doing one specific thing for a very long time and had stopped noticing that it did it.

Rowe breathed it once, testing, the way a man tests bathwater with two fingers before he commits the rest of his hand. He did not do it again. It was the Black Country on a still morning — a hundred chimneys agreeing on the same weather, coke-smoke and quenched iron, a smell he had walked out of an army recruiting office and straight back into eight years and one continent ago. He said nothing. Lyra had learned by then that the things worth his saying and the things he had words for did not reliably overlap.

Ardea walked at Lyra's other shoulder, scarf pulled over the lower half of her face against the highland cold, watching the road ahead with the specific posture of someone who has learned that cold does less damage if you decline to acknowledge it. Rowe fell in behind them both, watching the smoke — visible for the better part of an hour now, not rising from any single chimney but sitting over the next ridge as a general discoloration, a permanent grey-brown stain the overcast could not fully absorb.

[Beat 128]
The valley opened below them when the road crested the ridge.

Lyra stopped.

She was not a person who stopped for things. She had been trained out of it — the vigil's long-watch discipline, the Templar's field doctrine on maintaining movement in unfamiliar ground, seventeen years of applying both — and the discipline was sound, and she had followed it every day since. You cataloged as you moved. You did not give the landscape a performance of your reaction.

She stopped.

Below the ridge the valley dropped in a series of terraces, and on the largest of them, a league distant, stood a fortification. That was the correct word for its shape — corner towers, curtain wall, the thickened stone of a structure whose builders had assumed threat from every direction at once. The walls had kept something out for a long time, or kept something in, which was often the same wall doing the same job twice.

It was also, at the same time, a factory.

She had read the Compact's language for this — integrated manufacturing installation, castle-form adaptive reuse, Compact Year 1070, ongoing modification per Hearth Council decree — and had built an image from the words: a factory grown up beside a castle, or fitted inside one, some transitional state where you could still find the seam.

There was no seam. The curtain wall was the factory wall. The corner towers had been cored out and fitted with furnace stacks that rose straight up out of what had been the battlements, the old parapet stone now serving as collar-rings for iron chimneys thick as well shafts. Smoke stood over three of the four in a column that leaned southeast in the highland wind, steady enough that it had made its own local weather. The wall itself had been cut through in six places she could count from the ridge, openings wide enough to drive a loaded freight wagon through — not windows, not gates, just apertures sized to whatever the process needed moved in and out of it. Raw material entered on the east face. Finished product left on the west. The wall did its job as a factory wall and made no pretense of doing anything else.

This was not a castle with a mill grown up around it. It was a forge that had once needed walls, and had simply kept them.

There was nothing apologetic in it. That was the part she had not anticipated. The architecture did not excuse itself. The castle had been a castle. It was now also a forge. Both were true of the same stone, and the stone had no opinion either way.

"Year 1074," Orim said. "The Hearth Table authorized the first interior conversion under the manufacturing expansion protocols. Before that it was a garrison. Before the garrison, a watchtower against the valley floor. Before that, something the Atrament record won't discuss, which usually means there was an argument about who built it and nobody won."

"How old."

"The base courses read pre-Compact. Possibly pre-House. Someone was keeping watch from this ridge before the Houses existed." He looked at the stacks. "They keep a different watch from it now."

The sound resolved into its parts the closer they came: the repetitive thud of forging hammers at a cadence too even to be human hands; the deeper sustained note of furnaces at full heat; a rhythmic compression from somewhere inside the walls she couldn't place from outside. All of it running at once. All of it running without pause.

[Beat 129]
Below the fortification, in the yards between the outer perimeter and the approach road, the Heloths moved.

She had worked with Heloths at the Ocipheus Station — the vigil's courier models, lean single-rider constructs built to cross open ground without complaint. You developed a working sense of one if you rode it long enough, not fondness exactly, but familiarity with a machine built to do one thing well and decline everything else. The Heloths in the yards below were built for a different job: heavy-cargo variants, wide and low, the arm-housings fitted for cradle loads instead of saddle points, running the fixed routes between the factory mouths and the freight stage with the patient repetition of something calibrated to a task and content to run it until the maintenance interval said otherwise. Three stood motionless at what she recognized, even at this distance, as servicing stations.

Not all of it below her was Heloth. Between two of the cargo variants, drawn up in a ranked stillness at the yard's south wall, stood frames of a different make. Man-height. Man-proportioned, in a way no pure construct needed to be — articulated at shoulder, hip, and knee the way a body was articulated, not the way a machine found it convenient to move. She knew the type from the Vigil's own matériel briefings and had never before had it in front of her eyes: battle-rigs. Atrament's answer to the war it kept feeding. A Heloth was built whole, from the first rivet, to do a courier's job or a cargo job and nothing else. A battle-rig was built around something that had not started as metal. She did not know the process from this distance and did not, standing here, want the diagram. She knew the socketed housing at the sternum, sized for a spine, when she saw it, and she understood, on the ridge with the wind carrying the smoke southeast, what got made behind those six apertures and what it was made for. Verlaine took minds and gave them machine bodies to wear. Atrament took bodies — whoever's, wherever the supply was drawn from, and she filed that question next to the others she could not yet answer — and fused them into frames built to take a blow a living skeleton could not survive twice. Two Houses working the same war from opposite ends of the same problem. This was where one half of it got built, at the rate of however many hammer-strokes a minute the sound below the ridge was counting out.

The stations glowed. Orange, low, bioluminescent — the ember-fungus, introduced from some other Sphere generations back, that grew wherever the Catalyst discharge left its residue and did not stop growing once it had taken hold. She had read about it in the ecological annex at Ocipheus. Reading about a fungus that lit a construct yard orange from the inside was not the same thing as standing above one and watching it burn low and steady on stone that predated the Houses.

"At night, from a ridge, you can read the whole yard by the fungus alone," Orim said. "Every station. Every wall the discharge spatters during the cleaning cycle." He paused. "That will matter later."

"When."

"When we need to see the yards after dark," he said, in the tone of a man releasing information at the rate he judged useful and not one measure sooner.

[Beat 130]
The approach road ran through three settlements between the ridge and the Schmalburg basin, and none of them were towns.

The first was three long buildings and a dormitory block. "Hearth-stations," Orim said. "Numbered by production sequence, not geography. Station Twelve isn't near Eleven or Thirteen. It's the twelfth stage of the chain." He looked at the dormitory. "Same plan across the whole valley. Efficient use of the floor."

"It's very clean," Ardea said.

He looked at her.

"I was expecting it to be dirtier. Everything that size, making everything it's making. I expected more debris."

"Debris costs," Orim said. "Debris is raw material that has left the cycle before it should have. The Hearth Table worked out, four centuries ago, that the cheapest form of cleanliness is treating waste as escaped product. Nothing lands in that yard without a value assigned to it, and the value is recovered." He watched the clean yard slide past. "There are Atrament scholars who will tell you the arrangement isn't unkind at all — that it simply weighs collective output over individual comfort, and that the people living it understand the trade and accept it."

"Do they," Ardea said. "Accept it."

"The scholars," Orim said, "are not, as a rule, the people living it."

Ardea was quiet for a moment. "That's worse, somehow."

"Yes," Orim said, and did not elaborate, and Lyra understood that this was the whole of the answer he had.

At the second settlement they stopped briefly at the edge of a servicing yard, where a heavy Heloth stood under two workers running the discharge-clearing procedure on its back housing — the same one she knew from the Ocipheus maintenance cycle, scaled up. The ember-fungus washed the whole station orange, the workers visible in it as shapes more than as people. One said something short to the other. The other laughed, briefly, the ordinary currency of anyone doing a cold routine task in company.

Ardea watched them a moment longer than the pace of the road required, in the particular way she had of looking at things she meant to remember and had not yet found a place to file.

Then she moved on, and so did Lyra.

[Beat 131]
The staging ground was at the valley junction, where the approach road met the east-west freight road and the floor widened enough for a temporary military formation to sit comfortably: flat, sufficient, close to the eastern supply route without sitting on the rail line itself.

Six supply wagons under canvas. Three infantry columns standing at ease with their kit stacked at their feet, in the specific posture of men who had been told to wait and had been waiting long enough to develop opinions about the waiting. Two heavy Heloths moved between the columns carrying loads that were not exercise weight — she could read it in the gait, the small forward compensation of a construct carrying something denser than it had been trimmed for.

She counted the wagons twice. Six, both times.

The canvas was tied down under canonical property seals — the circle-and-line pressed in red wax, the Liturgy's mark for goods in transit under inter-House review. Property under that seal was subject to inspection only by the authority that had sealed it. Which meant it was subject to inspection by no one standing on this road.

She looked at the wagons. She looked at the columns. She looked at the direction the Heloths were carrying their loads — east, toward the highland approach, the route the Vigil rotation resupply used.

Seventeen years in the Ocipheus archive had put every disclosed Atrament production record filed under the Compact through her hands at least once. She knew the seasonal patterns: south to Loera for export, north to the Vigil line for resupply, held in the valley for the maintenance cycles. What she was looking at was neither. The Vigil line's own supply doctrine ran five sequential stages, and you did not open stage five until the first four were positioned and confirmed. Sealed wagons at a staging ground meant the beginning of nothing. They meant the end of something that had already been moving for a while — moving, she understood now, since well before any border stamp she or anyone else had carried across this line could have registered it. A front that relocated with the season made every notice provision in the Compact a formality already behind the ground it was meant to describe. That was not an oversight. That was the use of it.

She had been counting supply movement since the border without understanding the shape of it. She understood the shape of it now, standing at this junction, and it did not improve on understanding.

No Compact filing she had read disclosed an exercise here. No inter-House notice accounted for this supply chain. And the class of canonical seal on those wagons required countersignature from the Canon Immaculate — not the routine cross-border stamp, but the reservation kept for operations that needed the Liturgy's formal legal presence written into them before they happened.

She filed it, next to the theft, next to the Relic's absence, next to Prelacze's signature suspending her own field authority. Next to everything she did not yet have a whole shape for, whose edges were beginning, one at a time, to show themselves.

Rowe had stopped a little apart, watching the staging ground with the stillness she had learned to read as him keeping a record without a notebook. His face carried, for longer than the usual three seconds, the look of a man for whom the present had briefly become a transparency laid over something he already carried. The columns. The equipment loads. The faces of men who had been standing at ease since some hour that morning they hadn't chosen.

Then he was watching the road again, the way he always watched it, and she did not ask.

He spoke rarely, and when he did it was clipped. Wait. No. That way. And one further word she was fairly sure meant I understand, though he deployed it in contexts that also seemed to mean I have seen this before and, on at least one occasion, this is the same thing wearing different clothes. It carried a great deal of freight for one word.

Whatever the staging ground had told him, that word would not cover it. She let it settle, and they went on.

[Beat 132]
The descent toward Schmalburg took the rest of the afternoon, the road switchbacking down through the lower terraces until the basin opened beneath them: a river confluence silted, over centuries, into a flat harbor bottom, the dockyard running the western bank in old garrison stone for what looked, from above, like half a league. Atrament's machine yards had grown to meet the dockyard wall from the landward side, filling every open ground between it and the rail line, so that the wall was now less a boundary than a hinge between two kinds of the same industry.

The Veil ran brown below it. Not mud-brown. Not storm-brown. The particular brown of something introduced rather than carried — the color of a river that had been receiving the valley's discharge for generations and had stopped pretending to be anything else. She had read the numbers on this once, in an annex, and nodded at them the way anyone nods at a statistic that is bad but not present. The river from the ridge was present.

There was construction on the dockyard wall. Not repair — new work: scaffolding at three points along the western face, dressed stone going up above the old coursing, the mortar lines even and fine in a way the garrison work below them was not. This required a hand the Heloths didn't have.

Below the scaffolding, along the wall's base, there were workers.

Not construct handlers. Not Hearth-station labor in its dormitory rotation. These moved dressed stone from the staging pile to the scaffold foot, ran the rope-and-pulley that carried the larger blocks up to the men setting the courses above, with the steady, unremarkable efficiency of people for whom the work had long since stopped being anything to remark on — not automatic, but habituated the way difficult repeated labor becomes habituated, the body finding the rhythm of the task while the rest of a person goes somewhere else and waits it out.

Lyra looked at them.

She looked at the dressed stone. She looked at the mortar lines, the fitment, the way the new courses would hold. She looked at the rope-and-pulley and the staging pile organized with the settled order of a site that had been running long enough to find its own logic.

She looked at the workers again.

She looked away.

She did not go looking for a word for it. She filed it, the way she filed the Heloths and the battle-rigs and the wagons and the columns, in the space that was getting fuller by the hour and had not, so far, produced anything she could act on before nightfall.

[Beat 133]
Orim made the decision for her when she opened her mouth to ask about entering the city that evening.

"No," he said. "Not tonight." He looked down at the dockyard, the wall, the road. "We watch it from here first. A city under pre-deployment develops an awareness. It watches who walks in." He shrugged the pack from his shoulder. "We go down when we know what it's watching for."

She considered arguing. She did not argue.

They made camp in the scrub above the road, a low fire on dry wood Ardea found wedged in a rock crevice where last season's growth had dried in place, small enough to throw no visible smoke. The cold at that height, once the sun had gone, required acknowledgment rather than refusal — it settled into the joints with the particular attention of a host who knows the guests have nowhere else to be.

They ate. They said little.

Then the dark finished arriving, and Schmalburg stopped being a city visible in its stonework and became a city visible in its glow.

Every servicing station in the dockyard's machine yards — she began counting, lost the thread, began again, gave up somewhere past a dozen as the farthest points blurred together — stood outlined in the same low orange bioluminescence, the fungus grown thick enough now to draw the whole yard's shape in light: the routes between stations worn thin where traffic kept the growth down, the walls that took the worst of the discharge spatter burning brightest of all.

In that light, the scaffolding on the dockyard wall was still visible. The workers were still there — not directly lit, but caught in the reflected orange rising off the stations below, shapes moving stone, the pulley system still turning, the work continuing past the hour Atrament's own hearth-stations rang their shift bells.

She watched the mortar going into the new courses, one stone at a time, patient, exact.

She still did not have a word for it.

[Beat 134]
"Tell me what you have," Orim said, without turning from the city.

She told him. The sequence from the border to the junction — the supply movements she'd logged without understanding, the stage numbers, the canonical class on the seals, the Vigil doctrine she'd read seven years ago in an archive and hadn't thought of since, the frames on the yard floor that weren't Heloths.

He listened without interrupting. When she finished, he looked at the city a while longer.

"They were never looking for the Relic," he said.

"No."

"They knew where Pallor was taking it when they arranged the theft. The destination was part of the arrangement." He watched the ember-glow on the yards. "The theft gave the Canon a legal basis for moving sealed wagons into position for a Vigil-approach operation without inter-House notice. Recovering a Liturgy asset under canonical review required the Liturgy's field authority to go looking for it. That authority is what's sitting on the valley floor tonight."

"You keep calling it an asset."

"Because that's the word the file uses." He turned his cup once in his hands, not yet drinking. "It isn't accurate. An asset does something. The Oculus doesn't do anything — not the way a blade does something, or a wagon of Catalyst does something. It looks. Something struck an aperture onto the core of Sinter longer ago than any record will name, and it never closed. Every scry I have ever taken heals shut behind me the moment I let it go — the skin seals, the sight is gone, and I keep whatever I managed to remember of it before that fades too. The Oculus never healed. It never stopped looking. Seven hundred and forty-one years of the same unbroken sight, still open right now, this hour, on whatever is growing down there and getting larger while it grows. And a scry reaches time as well as place, so the same window holds what came before it — the thing's own birth, in Year 1312, and older still, colder, a Sinter that was never a crater at all. The Canon didn't lose a weapon he can wield. He lost the one eye in the Entos that was still open."

"Then why keep it in a vault instead of looking through it. If it only watches, watching is not a crime."

"Because a vault was never where you keep a thing safe," Orim said. "It's where you keep a thing from being used. The Liturgy has been the one authority both those armies down there will still accept, for three hundred years, precisely because it has never had to answer for what it's been filing under 'no action indicated' since Year 630. Read what it's seeing and someone has to explain the silence. Easier, every time, to lose the eye that never closes than to answer for what it's still watching."

"It also required suspending the Templar assigned to the case," she said. "My transit documents would have crossed those supply routes eventually. I'd have read the stage-five sequence for what it was."

"As you have."

"I was meant to be delayed long enough that the deployment finished before I could report it."

He turned his cup slowly in his hands. "That's the charitable read."

She said nothing.

"The uncharitable read," he said, "is that you were meant to be holding the Relic when this resolves. A Templar with seventeen years of documented loyalty to the Canon Immaculate, found in possession of a stolen artifact recovered from the House the Liturgy's own deployment is about to implicate, is useful to have on the record." He paused. "I mention it because it's true, not because it changes anything we're doing."

"It doesn't change anything we're doing."

"Then we go on as we were."

[Beat 135]
Rowe sat at the edge of the firelight, not quite in it, his rifle against the rock beside him, watching the valley with the stillness of a man keeping a record he had no way to write down. The ember-glow reached the tributary below the dockyard and lay orange on brown water — the specific color of this place, at this hour, doing what it had been doing for longer than any of them had been alive.

He said nothing. He had nothing yet to say it with. But he had been watching the construction on that wall a long time, and his face had settled into the look she was learning to read as recognition — not the three-second flash he gave to a familiar bend of river or a particular color of stone, but something that had come in and stayed.

She would not know what it was tonight. Perhaps not until he had enough Entos to give it to her. Perhaps not even then.

[Beat 136]
She stayed awake after the others slept, the cold a reason and the thinking the real one, and went through the sequence from the beginning: the stage-five movement, the seals, the countersignature and what it implied about timing, how far in advance an order like that had to be given, what the earlier stages must have looked like and whether she had already seen them and simply filed them wrong. She had. Three separate sightings, refiled now under their true heading. They fit.

The deployment had been running before the theft. The theft was not the start of anything — it was the last piece of a chain that was already moving, and her own chase, her own authorization, her own dispatch from Ocipheus, had been stage five of something set in motion before she'd known there was anything to set in motion.

She went through it again. It did not change.

The eastern approach was staged. The columns were positioned. The seal was countersigned, sealed under review provisions that put off any notice until the operation was already underway. All that remained was the order.

She looked at the ember-glow, steady and indifferent, fed by exhaust heat and discharge and a manufacturing night that never stopped to consider whether it should. She looked at the wall, the fine new stone going up in the dark, the workers still at it, the mortar setting in the cold. She looked once more, in her mind, at the frames drawn up along the south wall of the yard above them, waiting on whatever came next out of the sockets built into their chests, and understood that the columns waiting east on the highland road and the rigs waiting on the yard floor were the same appetite, fed from two different gates of the same fortress.

She looked east, toward the highland approach, where the columns waited for a word that had not yet come.

When the eastern sky began, by the barest degree the eye could not quite commit to, to separate itself from the highland above it, she put her hands in her pockets and looked once more at the valley — the brown water, the orange glow still holding its heat against the coming light — and did the last of her arithmetic for the night.

First light. The harbor. Whatever the city was watching for, they would know it by noon.

Ten days behind them. The rest of the road ahead. Schmalburg below, and beyond it, an army pointed in the direction she needed to go, waiting on an order that would arrive whether or not she was there to see it.

She went through the sequence once more, to be certain she had not missed anything.

She had not.

[Beat 137]
Vega set the instruments in order before the second candle burned down to its mark — three for the session itself (always three; a clean number, and it still pleased her), one for supplementary materials intake, one for the disposition record, one for archival certification. The sixth she left blank until the session's shape was known. A blank instrument was always better than the wrong one already written out — she liked that rule so much she had copied it into her own working stock.

Chamber Three was cold in the way of rooms that held important things infrequently. The stone kept its chill against itself like a resource, and spent it into the air in slow degrees — the brazier at the far wall was lit, but the ceiling ate the warmth before it ever reached the scribing table. She could feel her fingers. That was good. She could feel them precisely in the way that meant she would not feel them much longer if she sat still.

The convocation, which she had copied herself three days prior — some of the clauses were lovely, actually, in the dry way the canonical Received Tongue could surprise you — sat stamped and folded at the head of the table on the Canon Immaculate's side. Not because Prelacze would need it. In eight sessions she had learned he never referred to instruments once a session opened; he carried the citations in his head, the way she carried the sound of certain phrases she couldn't fully translate and loved anyway. It sat there because its position was procedurally correct, and Prelacze ran correct sessions. Not in question. Not yet.

The Canon arrived before the delegates. This, too, was correct — presiders did not enter after the people they presided over, and she had confirmed it across eight sessions running: he arrived at precisely the point where everything was nearly ready but the room had not yet finished being warmed. Deliberate, or just habit? Probably not, on balance. Not entirely certain, though. She kept a private, unwritten column for things she wasn't certain of, and this lived in it.

[Beat 138]
"Scribe Athen-Moor." He said it the way he said everything to her — acknowledgment of a correctly arranged room, not greeting. She had concluded some time ago that this was the most his tone would ever do in her direction, and that this was fine: she was furniture. Competent furniture.

"Canon." She kept her pen in hand.

He took his place at the table's head and reviewed the convocation without sitting — a man in his sixties who moved through institutional space the way certain rivers moved through stone: not rushing, not wasting effort, occupying whatever channel the room provided him. He read the convocation once, set it down at exactly its correct position, and said, "The Loera delegate will have prepared a brief. Allow her to finish reading it before you note the time." A pause, while he looked at the high window and its inch of grey light. "She has come three days by the coast road for this."

Vega wrote it on her working copy — allow delegate to complete reading — then crossed it out; it was not her assignment to record what the Canon anticipated, only what the session produced. A lovely instinct of his, she thought, looking at the crossed-out line a half-breath longer than the crossing-out required. But an instinct was not a promise. She filed the distinction and moved on.

The delegates arrived together, kept waiting in the approach corridor and brought in at the same time — standard form for a contested review; she liked that phrase, the two words leaning against each other like they weren't sure which should apologize first. She knew the Loera delegate from the registry files: Adnora Keist, nine years cataloguing the western strait approaches for the Three-Lantern House. Loera did not send delegates out of principle; it sent them when the shipping lanes were at risk, and half of Loera's trade ran through the western sector — the delegate's presence was itself a document, if you knew how to read arrivals the way you read instruments. The Carnyx commander she knew from his intake certification, filed six weeks past: Aldec Vorn, Carnyx Territorial Authority, Vigil sector eastern designation. He carried a scroll case with four rolls inside it. She knew the count without asking; she had processed the inventory certification herself.

They came in with the careful arrangement of people told to appear collaborative, who had each decided, separately, that this was not entirely the wrong thing to be — a kind of hope, in its narrow way.

She dated the session instrument. She noted the attendees. She wrote the Canon's name and designation. She prepared to copy what was said. Not what she suspected. What was said.

[Beat 139]
Keist read her brief in twelve minutes, written out in full as Prelacze had anticipated, without looking up — which was not what frustrated witnesses usually did. Frustrated witnesses spoke, and let their frustration do work the document should have been doing instead. Keist read. She had already decided her frustration would not stand in the canonical record. The document would. Vega found this almost unbearably admirable — a delegate who understood that much about archives was worth listening to twice.

The brief described the sector's deterioration in survey terms — seventeen points along the perimeter, measured at eight-month intervals across three years. Eleven of the seventeen fell outside the established baseline range. Four of the eleven exceeded the canonical threshold the Liturgy's own vigil standards defined as acceptable variation. Keist cited the document number. She cited the page. She cited the threshold value — three exact numbers in a row like a little chime, before Vega caught what the chime was telling her: a delegate who did not need to raise her voice was a delegate whose numbers were already doing the raising for her.

Vega copied all of it. The survey points. The variances. The citation. The threshold value. Her hand moved and her mind moved slightly ahead of her hand, the way it always did, cataloguing before she had quite decided to let it.

Prelacze listened without expression.

Vorn's presentation was shorter. He unrolled the maps across the table — she had cleared the space in anticipation, of course she had, that was the job — and said, "Eastern sector survey. Current against the fifteen-year baseline."

That was all he said. The maps were the presentation — its own flat soldier's eloquence: Vorn trusted the ink to argue for him the same way Keist trusted the citations, both arriving at the identical strategy from opposite houses.

She was not meant to study materials while they were presented; her station was to the side and slightly back, her assignment the record of what was said, not the documents themselves. But she noted two things anyway — noting was simply what her eyes did. In the central section of the eastern survey, the gap between the old line and the new one was wider than the station courtyard was long. And in three places along the southeastern passage, the new line had moved into ground that the old survey's own legend marked maintained transit corridor, canonical approach authorization required — four words that had sat quietly in a legend for fifteen years meaning one thing, and now, apparently, meaning another.

She looked at her table and wrote: eastern sector survey presented, four sheets, submitted for archival. Current measurement against fifteen-year baseline. See submitted materials.

She wrote what was there. That was her assignment, and she held to it the way she held to every rule that kept the room from asking more of her than it was owed.

[Beat 140]
"The Carnyx petition will have the maps entered into the session record," Prelacze said. He had not looked at them. He had been looking at the window. "Scribe."

"Canon."

"Noted in the record."

"Noted."

Keist said, in the flat voice of someone who had already spent her eloquence elsewhere: "The perimeter is not stable."

Prelacze turned from the window. "The Liturgy's vigil record spans seven hundred years," he said. "In that time it has documented thirty-one instances of boundary variation exceeding baseline threshold in one or more sectors. Twenty-eight self-corrected within twenty-four months. The remaining three showed continued movement over longer periods, and all three fell within the documented range for cyclical variation, per the vigil's own standards framework." A pause. "The current measurements fall within that range."

He said it without hesitation, the cadence of a man reciting something true — Vega half-admired the sentence before she caught what it was built to do: not to answer Keist, but to outlast her. She supposed it was true. She had not consulted the vigil record herself, and had no cause to. She wrote that thought down nowhere.

Keist said, "The transit corridors—"

"Are under review. The process for contested approach territories in Vigil-adjacent sectors requires formal assessment by the Order of the Watch's sector command, submitted to the canonical registry. That process has been initiated."

He did not say I initiated it. He said the process has been initiated. Two sentences wearing the same coat — one had a person in it, and one didn't, and the one without a person in it could never be asked, later, why the person had done what he did.

Vorn said nothing further. He rolled one of the maps back up with the care of a man who had decided the maps would do no further work here today.

"Acknowledged," Keist said. The word landed flat on the table and stayed there — one of the saddest words in the canonical vocabulary, Vega thought, because it cost the speaker nothing and cost everyone else everything.

Prelacze looked at her directly for a moment. "The Loera petition's documentation is noted in the record and will inform the sector review. This session has received and registered the presented materials." Without warmth, without coldness — the register of something institutional and final. "No immediate action is indicated. Continued monitoring protocols are authorized."

She wrote: no immediate action indicated. Continued monitoring protocols authorized.

She wrote: Loera and Carnyx concerns noted in the record.

She did not write: the maps are in the archival queue and the lines on them say the cavity is larger. That sentence sat behind her teeth the whole time she was writing the correct one. That was not her assignment. Her assignment was the record of what was determined.

[Beat 141]
The delegates left first. Keist did not look at Prelacze on her way out, which was a controlled thing to do — controlled things were so often the only kind available to people who lost. Vorn paused where the maps had been spread — already gathered into Vega's hands — looked at the empty table for a moment, and went.

Prelacze signed the instruments she presented at the table, not at corridor speed. He read each one before signing, unhurried, as he had never once been hurried about a canonical instrument in eight sessions. Virtue, or a very specific kind of confidence — she still hadn't decided which.

"The Loera delegate's documentation," he said, not looking up from the fourth. "Complete archival processing. Sector review intake will require cross-reference."

"I'll set it up."

He signed the fourth. He looked at the fifth. "The Carnyx materials."

"I have them."

"Archived without action."

"Yes, Canon."

He signed the fifth, handed the set back, and went.

[Beat 142]
She stood at the table with the instruments in one hand and the map case in the other. The brazier had been burning three hours and the room was warmer than it had been, though she could no longer feel it as warmth — her body had gone numb past the point of reporting heat honestly, and she trusted neither the numbness nor whatever came after it.

She sat. She set the instruments aside and opened the case.

The maps were good parchment, careful work — the kind of cartography that did not leave room to argue with the line, and she loved that about cartography the same helpless way she loved a well-drafted clause. The old survey was faded brown, hand-drawn, dated fifteen years back in the lower corner — before the secondary monitoring protocols, before the last border reclassification, before any of the paper she had spent three years handling. The new survey was clean black ink, same scale, same survey points, each marked with a small cross and a number that matched the measurement log on the second sheet. Triangulation references. Distances in standard canonical units. A stated margin of error, and a small one. Vorn's cartographers had not left room to argue with the line either.

In the northwestern sector, the current line sat two hundred and thirty meters east of the baseline. She checked the scale — always checked the scale — and the station courtyard, which she walked daily and knew the way she knew her own handwriting, would fit inside that gap once. Comfortably.

In the central sector, the gap was three hundred and eighty meters. The courtyard would fit twice.

In the southeastern passage — the one the old legend marked maintained transit corridor, canonical approach authorization required — the current line touched the corridor's inner boundary. Did not cross it. Touched it.

Touching was not crossing. She noted this honestly, because it was a meaningful distinction if you were building a canonical argument, and the maps were not building an argument. They had measured what was there and drawn what they found.

She rolled the first sheet closed, then the second, and set them in the case. In the disposition column she wrote: archived without action, per Canon Immaculate session determination. She sealed the case with the Registry stamp and placed it in the archival queue.

She picked up her pen and did not write anything for a moment.

She had been good at her work before she became too good at it. She was not certain when the two states had separated — somewhere in the second year, when she stopped seeing only the instruments and started seeing what they were about. A notary was not supposed to be about content. A notary was supposed to be about form: the correct certification, the correct stamp, the correct procedure. Her procedure was excellent. It was not her function to decide what was correct to do about a map that showed a cavity three hundred and eighty meters larger than it had been fifteen years ago. That was the Canon's function. He had exercised it. She had written it down.

She was very good at this.

[Beat 143]
She was in the corridor outside the administrative wing, carrying the session instruments toward the registry, when she heard them coming from the eastern approach.

Seven soldiers. Her mind catalogued them before she had decided to let it — three years processing complement logs and supply authorizations had left her with the reflex of reading equipment the way she read certification stamps. Heavy weather-treatment on the outer layers, Vigil-approach rated. Boot construction cut for the eastern approach roads — the exact design she had filed requisition instruments for herself. Kit profile she knew from evaluation summaries she'd been copying since the sixth month: Descent Corps issue. Not garrison — she knew the garrison faces, every one, and none of these belonged to that roster. Not Carnyx insignia either, though the route was the same eastern corridor Vorn had spent the afternoon arguing was measurably, provably wrong. Possibly Carnyx-routed. She was not certain, and she wrote it that way in her head before she wrote anything anywhere else — an uncertain fact recorded as a certain one was worse than no fact at all.

The soldiers had the look of people who had been traveling — not the soft exhaustion of a long, aimless road, but the alert exhaustion of purposeful movement: a brief, a destination, not yet the requirement to execute it.

She pressed herself to the wall to let them by. The corridor was narrow, and they went close enough that she could smell the road on them — cold and dust and the dry chill of high-ground travel. The last soldier looked at her as he passed. Not suspicion. The kind of awareness that clocked her, filed her, and moved on without breaking stride — he had catalogued her exactly the way she catalogued him, and she did not enjoy the symmetry.

She waited until they turned the corner. Then she went on to the registry.

[Beat 144]
The intake log for the specialist deployment was in the pending queue on her desk, delivered that morning, before the session. She picked it up.

SPECIALIST DEPLOYMENT — SINTER ZONE APPROACH PREPARATION.

She held the instrument. Seven names in the personnel column, standard roster format. Equipment certification attached. Authorization chain: a single reference number she did not immediately place — an unfamiliar series, which happened sometimes with restricted categories.
